\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\left[R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-R(\boldsymbol{\beta}|\boldsymbol{X})\right]-\left[R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\right]\right|\leq\varphi M+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\epsilon-\frac{c_{1}}{\log p},

and

\varphi=a_{1}\frac{\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+a_{2}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}\log p}{N^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}+a_{3}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+U_{\epsilon}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}(\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}}}.

Proof.

First, remark that, by the Taylor expansion, we have that for all
x,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
which satisfies

|\log(1+\exp(x+\varepsilon))-\log(1+\exp(x))|=\left|\frac{\exp(x^{\prime})}{1+\exp(x^{\prime})}\varepsilon\right|\leq|\varepsilon|.

This implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\left[R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-R(\boldsymbol{\beta}|\boldsymbol{X})\right]-\left[R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-R(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X})\right]\right|

(36)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\log\left(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)-\log\left(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\log\left(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right)-\log\left(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right|+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|.

Next, using the second statement of Lemma
B.1
, this leads to

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M}\left|\left[R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-R(\boldsymbol{\beta}|\boldsymbol{X})\right]-\left[R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-R(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X})\right]\right|

(37)

\displaystyle\leq\Omega_{*}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)M+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{G^{*}}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}M+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|.

Then, remark that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}

(38)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)+f_{i}(t)-\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]+\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]-\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(a1)+(a2)+(a3),

where

\displaystyle(a1)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty};

\displaystyle(a2)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\right|_{\infty};

\displaystyle(a3)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\right|_{\infty}.

First, we bound the term
(a1)
. By definition of
\widehat{f}_{i}(t)
and
f_{i}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\delta_{i}(t)}{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}-\frac{\delta_{i}(t)}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{1}{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\right|.

By standard properties of the Kaplan-Meier estimator
\widehat{H}
, (see Theorem 1.1 in
Gill (1983)
), it holds

\sup_{x\in[0,t]}|H(x)-\widehat{H}(x)|=O_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right),

(39)

where we used Assumption
2.2
. Moreover, by Assumption
2.2
, we have
H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)\geq C_{r}
, which leads to

\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N}\left|\frac{1}{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\right|=O_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right),

(40)

which means

\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N}\left|\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)\right|=O_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

(41)

Then, for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
U_{\epsilon}^{\prime}>0
such that

P\left(\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N}\left|\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)\right|\leq\frac{U_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\geq 1-\epsilon.

(42)

For all
k>0
, by Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|X_{i,j}\right|>k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq p}P\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|>k\right)

\displaystyle\leq Np\max_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq p}P\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|>k\right)

\displaystyle\leq Np\frac{K_{0}}{k^{q}},

since for all
i\in[N],\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq p}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|^{q}\right)\leq K_{0}
from Assumption
3.1
. Let
k=(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}
, then, we have

P\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq p}\left|X_{i,j}\right|>(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}\right)\leq Np\frac{\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq p}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|^{q}\right)}{NK_{0}p\log p}\leq\frac{1}{\log p}.

(43)

Combining (
42
) and (
43
), we have that for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
U_{\epsilon}=U_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\sqrt{G^{*}}K_{0}^{\frac{1}{q}}>0

\sqrt{G^{*}}\times(a1)\leq\sqrt{G^{*}}\times\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N}\left|\widehat{f}_{i}(t)-f_{i}(t)\right|\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{U_{\epsilon}p^{\frac{1}{q}}(\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}}}

(44)

holds with probability
1-\epsilon-\frac{1}{\log p}
.

Second, we consider the term
(a2)
. By Lemma
B.4
, there exists constants
C_{2},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}>0
such that the event

\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right)\right|_{\infty}\geq A_{1}\frac{\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+A_{2}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}\log p}{N^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}+A_{3}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}

(45)

holds with probability at most
C_{2}/\log p
.
Finally, concerning
(a3)
, we know that

\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]=\frac{\exp\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i}\right)}{1+\exp\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i}\right)},

and

\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]=\mathbbm{E}\left[\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\right]=\mathbbm{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\right]=\mathbbm{E}\left[\frac{\exp\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i}\right)}{1+\exp\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i}\right)}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right].

Applying Lemma
B.5
, we obtain that there exist constant
C_{3},A_{4},A_{5},A_{6}
such that the event

\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{E}[f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}]\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\mathbbm{E}\left[f_{i}(t)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right]\right|_{\infty}\geq A_{4}\frac{\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+A_{5}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}\log p}{N^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}+A_{6}\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}

(46)

holds with probability at most
C_{3}/\log p
.

The result follows by combining (
37
), (
38
), (
44
), (
45
) and (
46
), with
a_{1}=\sqrt{G^{*}}(A_{1}+A_{4})
,
a_{2}=\sqrt{G^{*}}(A_{2}+A_{5})
,
a_{3}=\sqrt{G^{*}}(A_{3}+A_{6})
,
c_{1}=1+C_{2}+C_{3}
.
∎

B.6
Relationship between population and sample effective sparsity

Now, we tie the population effective sparsity
\Gamma^{-2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\cdot)\right)
and the sample effective sparsity
\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\cdot)\right)
.

Lemma B.8
.

Under Assumption
3.1
, there exist universal constants
C_{4},B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}>0
such that

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)\geq\Gamma^{-2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)-U_{H_{1}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{4}}{\log p},

where
U_{H_{1}}=9G^{*}\left(B_{1}\frac{\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+B_{2}\frac{p^{\frac{2}{q}}\log p}{N^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}+B_{3}\frac{p^{\frac{2}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})\big)^{2}}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}
and
\Sigma=\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]
. We have

\displaystyle\left|\Delta^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}\Delta\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\Delta^{\top}\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\Delta\right|-\left|\Delta^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right)\Delta\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\Delta^{\top}\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\Delta\right|-G^{*}\Omega^{2}\left(\Delta\right)\left|\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right|_{\infty},

where we used that, by the first and third statements of Lemma
B.1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Delta^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right)\Delta\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Omega\left(\Delta\right)\Omega_{*}\left(\left(\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right)\Delta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Omega\left(\Delta\right)G^{*}\left|\left(\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right)\right|_{\infty}\Omega\left(\Delta\right)

\displaystyle=G^{*}\Omega^{2}\left(\Delta\right)\left|\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right|_{\infty}.

Remark that, for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\Omega^{+}(\Delta)=1
and
\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 2
, we have
\Omega\left(\Delta\right)=\Omega^{+}(\Delta)+\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 3
and, therefore,

\left|\Delta^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}\Delta\right|\geq\left|\Delta^{\top}\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\Delta\right|-9G^{*}\left|\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right|_{\infty}.

By Lemma
B.6
, we have

P\left(9G^{*}\left|\widehat{\Sigma}-\mathbbm{E}\left[\widehat{\Sigma}\right]\right|_{\infty}\leq U_{H_{1}}\right)\geq 1-\frac{C_{4}}{\log p}.

Then, by definition
\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)
and
\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)=\mathbbm{E}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)\right)
, we obtain, for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\Omega^{+}(\Delta)=1
and
\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 2
, that

P\left[\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)\geq\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)-U_{H_{1}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{4}}{\log p}.

(47)

Recall the definition of (
24
) and minimizing both the
\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)
and
\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)
with respect to
\{\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\text{$\Omega^{+}(\Delta)=1$ and $\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 2$}\}
, we obtain

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)\geq\Gamma^{-2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot))-U_{H_{1}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{4}}{\log p}.

∎

Lemma B.9
.

Under Assumption
3.1
, there exist universal constants
C_{5},B_{4},B_{5},B_{6}>0
such that, for all
M\geq 0
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that
\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M
,

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\cdot))\geq\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)-U_{H_{2}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p},

where

U_{H_{2}}=18(G^{*})^{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\left(B_{4}\frac{\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+B_{5}\frac{p^{\frac{2}{q}}\log p}{N^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}+B_{6}\frac{p^{\frac{2}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}\right)+K_{0}^{\frac{2}{q}}\right)M(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Furthermore, it holds that

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}(\cdot)\right)\geq\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)-U_{H_{2}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p}.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\Sigma}_{M}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}}{\mathrm{C}_{M}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})}
and
\widehat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})\big)^{2}}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}
. For any
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\Omega^{+}(\Delta)=1
and
\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Delta^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{M}\Delta\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\Delta^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}\Delta\right|-\left|\Delta^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{M}-\widehat{\Sigma}\right)\Delta\right|.

Since

\displaystyle\left|\Delta^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{M}-\widehat{\Sigma}\right)\Delta\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}_{M}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})}-\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})\big)^{2}}\right)\Delta^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\Delta\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}_{M}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})}-\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})\big)^{2}}\right|\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\Delta\right|.

Moreover, letting
A_{i}=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i}
,
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}_{M}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})}-\frac{\exp(A_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(A_{i})\big)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}_{M}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})}-\left(\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}+M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\left(\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}+M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}\right)-\frac{\exp(A_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(A_{i})\big)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left(\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}+M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}-M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\left(\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}+M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}-M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}\right|+\left|\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}+M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)}-\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(A_{i}\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|M\Omega_{*}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right|,

where the last inequality comes from that, for all
x,\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
x^{\prime}
satisfying

\left|\frac{1}{1+\exp(x+e)}-\frac{1}{1+\exp(x)}\right|=\left|\frac{-\exp(x^{\prime})}{(1+\exp(x^{\prime}))^{2}}e\right|\leq|e|,

by a Taylor expansion.
For all
k>0
, by Markov’s inequality, we see that

\displaystyle P\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|X_{i,j}\right|>k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq p}P\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|>k\right)

\displaystyle\leq Np\max_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq p}P\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|>k\right)

\displaystyle\leq Np\frac{\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq j\leq p}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|^{q}\right)}{k^{q}},

since for all
i\in[N],\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq p}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|^{q}\right)\leq K_{0}
by Assumption
3.1
. Let
k=(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}
. Then, we have

P\left(\max_{i\in[N],j\in[p]}\left|X_{i,j}\right|>(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}\right)\leq Np\frac{\max\limits_{i\in[N],j\in[p]}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}\right|^{q}\right)}{K_{0}Np\log p}\leq\frac{1}{\log p}.

(48)

By the second statement of Lemma
B.1
and (
48
), we obtain that

\max_{i\in[N]}\Omega_{*}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)\leq\max_{i\in[N]}\sqrt{G^{*}}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{G^{*}}(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}

(49)

holds with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{\log p}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}_{M}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})}-\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{\big(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})\big)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{i\in[N]}M\Omega_{*}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)

(50)

\displaystyle\leq 2M\sqrt{G^{*}}(K_{0}Np\log p)^{\frac{1}{q}}

holds with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{\log p}
.

As for
\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\Delta\right|
, since
\Omega^{+}(\Delta)=1
and
\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 2
, we can see
\Omega\left(\Delta\right)=\Omega^{+}(\Delta)+\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 3
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\Delta\right|

(51)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}-\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\right)\right)\Delta+\Delta^{\top}\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\right)\Delta\right|

\displaystyle\leq G^{*}\Omega^{2}(\Delta)\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}-\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\right)\right|_{\infty}+\max_{i\in[N]}G^{*}\Omega^{2}(\Delta)\left|\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\right)\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 9G^{*}\left(B_{4}\frac{\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+B_{5}\frac{p^{\frac{2}{q}}\log p}{N^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}+B_{6}\frac{p^{\frac{2}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}\right)+9G^{*}K_{0}^{\frac{2}{q}},

holds with probability at least
1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}
for some constants
B_{4},B_{5},B_{6}>0
. The first inequality comes from the third statement of Lemma
B.1
. The first part of the last inequality is obtained from Lemma
B.7
and the second part is from Assumption
3.1
, which implies
\max\limits_{1\leq j,l\leq p}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}X_{i,l}\right|\right)\leq\max\limits_{1\leq j,l\leq p}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|X_{i,j}X_{i,l}\right|^{\frac{q}{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}\leq K_{0}^{\frac{2}{q}}
.

Combining (
50
) and (
51
), we conclude that

\left|\Delta^{\top}\left(\widehat{\Sigma}_{M}-\widehat{\Sigma}\right)\Delta\right|\leq U_{H_{2}}

holds with probability at least
1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p}
.
By the same argument as in Lemma
B.8
, we have

P\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)\geq\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)-U_{H_{2}}\right)\geq 1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p}.

(52)

By definition of
\widehat{\tau}_{M}^{2}(\Delta)
, for all
\boldsymbol{\beta}
satisfying
\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M
, we have
\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{2}(\Delta)\geq\widehat{\tau}_{M}^{2}(\Delta)
. It follows

P\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{2}(\Delta)\geq\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{2}\left(\Delta\right)-U_{H_{2}}\right)\geq 1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p}.

(53)

Minimizing both left and right sides of the events in (
52
) and (
53
) with respect to
\{\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\text{$\Omega^{+}(\Delta)=1$ and $\Omega^{-}(\Delta)\leq 2$}\}
, we obtain

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}(\cdot)\right)\geq\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)-U_{H_{2}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p},

and

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(\cdot))\geq\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)-U_{H_{2}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{5}}{\log p}-\frac{1}{\log p}.

∎

Combining Lemmas
B.8
and
B.9
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma B.10
.

Under Assumption
3.1
, for all
M\geq 0
, we have

P\left[\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}(\cdot)\right)\geq\Gamma^{-2}(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot))-U_{H_{1}}-U_{H_{2}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{C_{6}}{\log p},

where
U_{H_{1}},U_{H_{2}},
and
C_{6}=C_{4}+C_{5}+1
are defined in Lemmas
B.8
and
B.9
.

Finally, we have the following result.

Lemma B.11
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
and
3.2
hold. For all
M\geq 0
, if

(U_{H_{1}}+U_{H_{2}})\frac{s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}\leq\frac{1}{2},

then

P\left(\Gamma^{2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}(\cdot)\right)\leq\frac{2s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}\right)\geq 1-\frac{C_{6}}{\log p},

where
U_{H_{1}},U_{H_{2}},
and
C_{6}=C_{4}+C_{5}+1
are defined in Lemmas
B.8
and
B.9
.

Proof.

We work on the event

\mathcal{E}=\left\{\Gamma^{-2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}(\cdot)\right)\geq\Gamma^{-2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)-U_{H_{1}}-U_{H_{2}}\right\},

which has probability at least
1-\frac{C_{6}}{\log p}
by Lemma
B.10
. Remark that, by Lemma
B.2
, we know that
\Gamma^{2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)\leq\frac{s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}
, so that
(U_{H_{1}}+U_{H_{2}})\Gamma^{2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}
since
(U_{H_{1}}+U_{H_{2}})\frac{s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
by assumption.
Therefore, on
\mathcal{E}
, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma^{2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M}(\cdot)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Gamma^{2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)}{1-(U_{H_{1}}+U_{H_{2}})\Gamma^{2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Gamma^{2}\left(\tau_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}(\cdot)\right)\leq\frac{2s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}.

This yields the result.
∎

B.7
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Let

M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}=18\frac{\lambda s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}+\lambda^{-1}\frac{24}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}

and

\mathbbm{B}_{local}=\left\{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\right\}

be a convex neighborhood of the target parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
.
Define
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:=d\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+(1-d)\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
, where
d:=\frac{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}
.
We have

\displaystyle\Omega\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(d\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+(1-d)\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)=d\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

(54)

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}},

which shows that
\Omega\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
.

The rest of the proof shows that
\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
with large probability. Since
R_{N}(\cdot)+\lambda\Omega(\cdot)
is convex and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is the minimizer of (
5
), we get

\displaystyle R_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})+\lambda\Omega(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle\leq dR_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})+d\lambda\Omega(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})+(1-d)R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})+(1-d)\lambda\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

(55)

\displaystyle\leq R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})+\lambda\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right).

Adding the term
\left(R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\right)
on both sides of (
55
), we obtain

\displaystyle R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)

(56)

\displaystyle\leq-\left[\left(R_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})\right)-\left(R_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\right)\right]+\lambda\Omega\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)-\lambda\Omega(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}).

By definition of
\Omega(\cdot),\Omega^{+}(\cdot)
and
\Omega^{-}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle\Omega(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\Omega(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle=\Omega^{+}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})+\Omega^{-}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\Omega^{+}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\Omega^{-}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

(57)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{+}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\Omega^{+}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\Omega^{-}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle=\Omega^{+}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\Omega^{+}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)-\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right).

Let us define the event

\mathcal{A}=\left\{R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{2}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}+\lambda\left\{\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)-\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right\}\right\}.

By Theorem
B.2
, together with Assumption
3.3
, (
56
) and (
57
), since
\varphi=O\left(p^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}/N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}}\right)\lesssim\lambda
, we have
P\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\to 1.

Let also

\mathcal{B}=\left\{\Gamma^{2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\cdot\right)\right)\leq\frac{2s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}\right\}.

Note that, by Lemma
B.11
and Assumption
3.3
which implies that
s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}U_{H_{1}}=o(1)
and
\frac{s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}U_{H_{1}}+s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}U_{H_{2}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}\leq 1/2
,
we have

P\left(\mathcal{B}\right)\to 1
.

Let us introduce the event

\mathcal{C}=\left\{\lambda\Omega\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\frac{9\lambda^{2}s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{2\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}+\frac{\lambda}{4}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{6}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}\right\}.

We will show that
\mathcal{C}
holds almost surely on the event
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
. To do so, we work on
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
and consider two cases.

Case
1
.

In this case, we assume that
\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{2}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}.

Since we work on the event
\mathcal{A}
, we obtain

\lambda\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)+R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X})\leq 2\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{4}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}.

Since
R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\geq 0
by Lemma
B.3
, this yields

\lambda\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda}{6}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{4}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}.

Using
\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{2}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}
, we obtain that,

\lambda\Omega\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda}{4}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{6}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}.

(58)

so that
\mathcal{C}
holds.

Case
2
.

In this case, we assume that we assume that
\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)>\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{2}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}.

Since we work on
\mathcal{A}
, we obtain

\displaystyle R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)+\lambda\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|+\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

(59)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right).

Since
R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\geq 0
by Lemma
B.3
, we get
\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq 2\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)
. By definition of the effective sparsity, we obtain

\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\Gamma\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\cdot\right)\right).

Then adding
\frac{1}{2}\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)
on both sides of (
59
) and using the inequality
|ab|\leq(a^{2}+b^{2})/2
, we get

\displaystyle R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)+\lambda\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

(60)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\Gamma\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\cdot\right)\right)+\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\widehat{\tau}^{2}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}{2}+\frac{9}{8}\lambda^{2}\Gamma^{2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\cdot\right)\right)+\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}2|E_{i}|.

Since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathbbm{B}_{local}
, by Lemma
B.3
, we know that

R(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|\boldsymbol{X})-R\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|\boldsymbol{X}\right)\geq\frac{\widehat{\tau}^{2}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}{2}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\lambda\Omega^{-}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\Omega^{+}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

(61)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{8}\lambda^{2}\Gamma^{2}\left(\widehat{\tau}_{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}\left(\cdot\right)\right)+\frac{\lambda}{12}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{2}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}.

We obtain that
\mathcal{C}
holds since we work on
\mathcal{B}
.

We have now shown that
\mathcal{C}
holds on
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
. Thus, we obtain that
P\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\to 1
since
P(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B})\to 1.

Now, let us work on the event
\mathcal{C}
. On
\mathcal{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

(62)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9\lambda s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{2\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}+\frac{\frac{6}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{4\lambda}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\frac{1}{4}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}.

Since

\frac{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)}\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq\frac{M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}}{2}\\
\Leftrightarrow 2\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}+\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right),

it holds that

P\left(\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\leq M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\right)\to 1.

Furthermore, using Lemma
B.1
, we conclude that with probability going to
1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\widehat{P}(\boldsymbol{z})-P(\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)}{1+\exp\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)}-\frac{\exp\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+e\right)}{1+\exp\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+e\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}-e\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right|+\left|e\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Omega_{*}\left(\boldsymbol{x}\right)\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)+\left|e\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\lambda s_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|_{\infty}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{H}}}+\lambda^{-1}\left|\boldsymbol{x}\right|_{\infty}\frac{1}{N}|\boldsymbol{E}|_{1}+|e|,

where the first inequality comes from that, for all
x,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
x^{\prime}
satisfying

\left|\frac{\exp(x+\varepsilon)}{1+\exp(x+\varepsilon)}-\frac{\exp(x)}{1+\exp(x)}\right|=\left|\frac{\exp(x^{\prime})}{(1+\exp(x^{\prime}))^{2}}\varepsilon\right|\leq|\varepsilon|,

by a Taylor expansion. The last inequality comes from the second statement of Lemma
B.1
and the value of
M_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
.

Appendix C
Inference theory

C.1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We begin with the de-sparsified sparse-group LASSO estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{(\mathcal{J})}
. Recall the definition of
\dot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})
in (
26
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{(\mathcal{J})}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,(\mathcal{J})}

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{(\mathcal{J})}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,(\mathcal{J})}\right)-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\dot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right]

(63)

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{(\mathcal{J})}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,(\mathcal{J})}\right)-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\dot{R}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})+\ddot{R}_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\dot{R}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right]-\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right)_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\ddot{R}_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right]\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right),

where we use the mean value theorem and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is between
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
, and
\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
denotes the
p\times p
identity matrix.

To simplify the notation, let

\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i},

(64)

and

\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-f_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}.

(65)

With this notation, we can rewrite the previous expression (
63
) as

\displaystyle N^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{(\mathcal{J})}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0,(\mathcal{J})}\right)

\displaystyle=-N^{1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\dot{R}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right]

(66)

\displaystyle-N^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\right)\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-N^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})

\displaystyle-N^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right)_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle-N^{1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\ddot{R}_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right]\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right).

To establish the asymptotic normality of the de-sparsified estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{(\mathcal{J})}
, we decompose the right-hand side of (
66
) into five separate components:

\displaystyle tt_{1}

\displaystyle:=N^{1/2}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\dot{R}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right]\right|_{\infty},

(67)

\displaystyle tt_{2}

\displaystyle:=N^{1/2}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right|_{\infty},

\displaystyle tt_{3}

\displaystyle:=N^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})

\displaystyle tt_{4}

\displaystyle:=\left|N^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right)_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right|_{\infty},

\displaystyle tt_{5}

\displaystyle:=\left|N^{1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\ddot{R}_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right]\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right|_{\infty}.

Theorem
3.2
is a direct result of the following Lemmas
C.1
,
C.2
which show

tt_{1}=o_{P}(1),tt_{2}=o_{P}(1),tt_{3}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}),tt_{4}=o_{P}(1),tt_{5}=o_{P}(1),

and Slutsky’s lemma.

C.2
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma C.1
.

Given a fixed group
\mathcal{J}\subseteq[p]
. Under the conditions of Theorem
3.1
and Assumptions
3.4
,
3.5
,
3.6
, and
3.7
, the following results hold:

tt_{1}=o_{P}(1),tt_{2}=o_{P}(1),tt_{4}=o_{P}(1),tt_{5}=o_{P}(1).

Proof.

We first show that
tt_{1}=o_{P}(1)
. By substituting the definitions of

\dot{R}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})
and
\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})
from (
26
) and (
64
), we obtain

\displaystyle tt_{1}

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\Bigg|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})}-\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\Bigg|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\Bigg|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i})}{\left(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i})\right)^{2}}E_{i}\,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\Bigg|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i}\left(\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i})}{\left(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i})\right)^{2}}E_{i}\right)^{2}}{N}}\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)^{2}}{N}}

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\left\|\boldsymbol{E}\right\|_{N}\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)^{2}}{N}}

\displaystyle=o_{P}(1).

Here, the first equality follows from the mean-value theorem and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{i}
is between
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i}
, the first inequality is a consequence of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and the second inequality uses the bound

\frac{\exp(\cdot)}{(1+\exp(\cdot))^{2}}\leq 1
. Finally, the conclusion

tt_{1}=o_{P}(1)
follows from Assumption
3.5
(i), which implies

\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{N}=o_{P}(1/N^{1/2+1.5/q})
, together with Lemma
C.3
,
which establishes that
\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}\right|=O_{P}(N^{1.5/q})
.

Second, we show that
tt_{2}=o_{P}(1)
. To see this, we start by writing

\displaystyle tt_{2}

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right|_{\infty}

(68)

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{1}\left|\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{1}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}.

Next, we observe that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\mathbbm{E}(f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}

which corresponds to the term in (
38
). We then have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)+\mathbbm{E}(f_{i}(t)|\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(\frac{p^{\frac{1}{q}}(\log p)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}}}\right),

(69)

where the rate comes from the analysis of (
38
) in Theorem
B.2
.
Combining (
69
), Lemma
3.1
, and Assumption
3.6
, we conclude that

tt_{2}=o_{P}\left(\frac{1}{N^{1/8+1/(2q)}(\log p)^{1/q}}\right)=o_{P}(1).

Third, we consider
tt_{4}
. By definition, we have

\displaystyle tt_{4}

\displaystyle=\left|N^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\right)_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right|_{\infty}

(70)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|N^{1/2}\left(\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-e_{j}\right)\right|_{\infty}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right|_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|N^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-e_{j}\right)\right|_{\infty}\,\Omega\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=o_{P}(1),

where
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
has
1
in the
j
-th element and
0
for others.
Here, the first inequality follows from the first statement of Lemma
B.1
, by taking the norm as
|\cdot|_{1}
. The second inequality uses the equivalence

\Omega(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\sim|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}|_{1}
, which holds since the size of the group
\mathcal{J}
is fixed and
\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
by definition. The last equality in (
70
) follows from Lemma
C.4
, Theorem
3.1
, Assumption
3.6
, and
s^{*}\geq 1
, which provide the sufficient conditions to establish the stated order.

Lastly, we analyze
tt_{5}
. By definition, we have

\displaystyle tt_{5}

\displaystyle=\left|N^{1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left[\ddot{R}_{N}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\ddot{R}_{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right]\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right|_{\infty}

(71)

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\max_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\exp(\theta_{i})(1-\exp(\theta_{i}))}{(1+\exp(\theta_{i}))^{3}}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\max_{i\in[N],j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\exp(\theta_{i})(1-\exp(\theta_{i}))}{(1+\exp(\theta_{i}))^{3}}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}\max_{i\in[N],j\in\mathcal{J}}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\frac{\exp(\theta_{i})(1-\exp(\theta_{i}))}{(1+\exp(\theta_{i}))^{3}}\right|\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2}N^{1.5/q}\left\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right\|_{N}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq N^{1/2+1.5/q}\lambda^{2}s^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}},

where the first inequality is justified by the mean-value theorem and
\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\right|\leq\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}\right|,

the fourth inequality comes from Lemma
C.3
and the bound

\left|\frac{\exp(\theta_{i})(1-\exp(\theta_{i}))}{(1+\exp(\theta_{i}))^{3}}\right|\leq 1,

and the last inequality is justified by Lemma
D.4
. Under the rate conditions in Assumption
3.6
, this is sufficient to conclude that

tt_{5}=o_{P}(1).

∎

Lemma C.2
.

Given a fixed group
\mathcal{J}\subseteq[p]
. Under the conditions of Theorem
3.1
and Assumptions
3.4
,
3.5
,
3.6
, and
3.7
, there exists a covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}
, such that

\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}).

Furthermore, let
\left(\widetilde{T}_{(2)},\delta_{(2)},\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\right)
denote an independent copy drawn from the same distribution as the sample
\left\{\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right),i\in[N]\right\}
and

\xi_{KM,i}=\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\frac{\delta_{(2)}(t)\,\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\leq t\}}{H^{2}(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)})}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i}\right),

where
v(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},\cdot)
is the influence function of the Kaplan-Meier estimator of
H
for observation
i
and for any positive value
z

v\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},z\right)=H(z)\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq z,\delta_{i}=0\right\}}{\bar{P}\left(\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\int_{0}^{z\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}}\frac{P\left(u\leq\widetilde{T}\leq u+du,\delta=0\right)}{\bar{P}^{2}(u)}\right],

where
\bar{P}(u)=P\left(\widetilde{T}>u\right)
.
We then have

\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}Var\left(\left(-f_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}+\xi_{KM,i}\right)\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\top}.

Proof.

We decompose
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})
into two components:

A_{1}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})-\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\right),\quad A_{2}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\rho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}).

Our objective is to show that
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})
can be expressed as a U-statistic plus a negligible remainder term.

To this end, we first consider
A_{1}
.

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(f_{i}(t)-\widehat{f}_{i}(t)\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}\left(\frac{1}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\frac{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}+\frac{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}\frac{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\left(\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}O_{P}(N^{-1})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\left(\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\left(\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)\right)+O_{P}(N^{-1}),

where the second-to-last equality follows from (
39
) and (
40
), and the last equality relies on the facts that
\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}
is bounded,
1/H^{2}(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i})
is bounded by Assumption
2.2
, and that, for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=O_{P}(1),

which follows from Assumption
3.5
(iv) implying
\left(\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|\right)\right)^{q}\leq\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|^{q}\right)=O\left(1\right)
and Markov’s inequality. Combining these results, it follows that for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
,

\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}{H^{2}(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i})}=O_{P}(1).

(72)

Then, by Theorem 1 of
Lo and Singh (1986a)
, we obtain

\widehat{H}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)-H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{k},\delta_{k},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)+o_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right),

(73)

where for any positive value
z

\displaystyle v\left(\widetilde{T}_{k},\delta_{k},z\right)=H(z)\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\widetilde{T}_{k}\leq z,\delta_{k}=0\right\}}{\bar{P}\left(\widetilde{T}_{k}\right)}-\int_{0}^{z\wedge\widetilde{T}_{k}}\frac{P_{1}(du)}{\bar{P}^{2}(u)}\right].

(74)

Here,
\bar{P}(u)=P\left(\widetilde{T}>u\right)
and
P_{1}(du)=P\left(u\leq\widetilde{T}\leq u+du,\delta=0\right)
.
Let

\rho_{i,k}=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{k},\delta_{k},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right),

so that we can write

A_{1}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\rho_{i,k}+o_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right)+O_{P}(N^{-1}),

where we use (
72
) and (
73
).

Next, consider the term
A_{2}
. We define

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(-f_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i},

where

\phi_{i}=\left(-f_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}.

Hence, combining
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, we have

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})=A_{1}+A_{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left(\rho_{i,k}+\phi_{i}\right)+o_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right)+O_{P}(N^{-1}).

We now show that
A_{1}+A_{2}
can be rewritten as a symmetric sum plus a negligible term. To proceed, we define the following quantities:

\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho_{i,k}+\phi_{i}+\rho_{k,i}+\phi_{k}\right).

Note that

\displaystyle A_{1}+A_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}+o_{P}(N^{-1/2})+O_{P}(N^{-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,i}+o_{P}(N^{-1/2})+O_{P}(N^{-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N^{2}-N}\left(1-\frac{1}{N}\right)\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,i}+o_{P}(N^{-1/2})+O_{P}(N^{-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N^{2}-N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}-\frac{2}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,i}+o_{P}(N^{-1/2})+O_{P}(N^{-1}).

We first bound the diagonal term

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,i}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\rho_{i,i}+\phi_{i}\right).

For each
j\in G
,
\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\phi_{i}\right|\right)
is bounded since we have

\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\phi_{i}\right|\right)\lesssim\left(\mathbbm{E}\left(\left|\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right|^{q}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}=O\left(1\right)

by Jensen’s inequality, Assumptions
2.2
and
3.5
(iv). Obviously,
Var\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\phi_{i}\right)
is also bounded.
Then Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}=O_{P}(1).

(75)

Recalling the definition (
74
), it is immediate that
v_{i}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)
is uniformly bounded, since
H\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)\leq 1
by definitions and
\inf_{s\leq u\leq t}P(\widetilde{T}\geq u\mid\widetilde{T}\geq s)\geq C_{r}
by Assumption
2.2
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i,i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)

(76)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(N^{-1}\right),

where we use (
72
).
Hence, combining (
75
) and (
76
), we have

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,i}=O_{P}\left(N^{-1}\right).

(77)

Next, we bound the off-diagonal term:

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i}\sum_{k}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}-\frac{1}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i}\tilde{\xi}_{i,i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i}\sum_{k}\left(\rho_{i,k}+\phi_{i}\right)-O_{P}\left(N^{-2}\right).

Recalling (
39
) and (
73
), we have
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{k},\delta_{k},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)=O_{P}(N^{-1/2})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\rho_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}\times\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{k},\delta_{k},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)

(78)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right)\times\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right),

where the final equality follows from (
72
).

Combining (
75
) and (
78
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N^{2}-N)}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}^{\top}\sum_{i}\sum_{k}\left(\rho_{i,k}+\phi_{i}\right)-O_{P}(N^{-2})

(79)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(N^{-3/2}\right).

We can now conclude that by (
77
) and (
79
):

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})=A_{1}+A_{2}=\frac{2}{N^{2}-N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}+o_{P}\left(N^{-1/2}\right),

(80)

which can be expressed as a U-statistic plus a term of order
o_{P}(N^{-1/2})
.

Applying the classical Central Limit Theorem for U-statistics
(e.g., Theorem 1.1 of
Bose and Chatterjee (2018)
), we obtain

\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{2}{N^{2}-N}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq N}\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}\right),

(81)

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\mathbbm{E}\left(\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}\right)
.

We next examine the variance term
\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}
appearing above. To define the Hoeffding projection of the U-statistic kernel, we follow the
standard convention in the U-statistics literature.
Let
\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},E_{(1)}\right\}
and

\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)},\widetilde{T}_{(2)},\delta_{(2)},E_{(2)}\right\}

denote two independent copies drawn from the same distribution as the sample
\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},v(\cdot),E_{i},i\in[N]\right\}
, and let
\tilde{w}_{(1)}=\frac{\delta_{(1)}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{(1)}\leq t\}}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(1)}\right)}
and
\tilde{w}_{(2)}=\frac{\delta_{(2)}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\leq t\}}}{H^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)}
. With the notation, the first-order projection of the kernel

\tilde{\xi}_{i,k}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\tilde{\xi}_{(1),(2)}\mid\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}\tilde{w}_{(1)}\mathbbm{E}\left(v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(2)},\delta_{(2)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(1)}\right)\bigg|\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(-f_{(1)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\bigg|\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\left(-f_{(2)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(2)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(2)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-f_{(1)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\bigg|\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right),

where we use that

\mathbbm{E}\!\left(v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(2)},\delta_{(2)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(1)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)=0

(82)

which comes from the discussion under Theorem 2 in
Lo and Singh (1986a)
or the asymptotic property of the Kaplan–Meier estimator,
and

\mathbbm{E}\!\left[\!\left(-f_{(2)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(2)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(2)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\!\right]=\boldsymbol{0},

as established in (
27
).
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\tilde{\xi}_{(1),(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(-f_{(1)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\!\left[\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0}

since

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\!\left[\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\!\left[\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)},\widetilde{T}_{(2)},\delta_{(2)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}\,\mathbbm{E}\!\left[v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)},\widetilde{T}_{(2)},\delta_{(2)}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0},

where the last inequality comes from (
82
) by flipping the indices.
It follows that

\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{0}.

(83)

Hence, the centered version of the first projection of the U-statistic is given by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left(\tilde{\xi}_{(1),(2)}\mid\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)-\mathbbm{E}\left(\tilde{\xi}_{(1),(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-f_{(1)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\bigg|\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right).

Then we apply Theorem 1.1 of
Bose and Chatterjee (2018)
, the variance of the U-statistic is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle=4\,\mathrm{Var}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\left(-f_{(1)}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{(1)})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\,\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\tilde{w}_{(2)}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\,\mathrm{Var}\left(\left(-f_{i}(t)+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+E_{i})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}+\xi_{KM,i}\right)\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\top},

where the last equality comes from the fact that
\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{(1)},\widetilde{T}_{(1)},\delta_{(1)},E_{(1)}\right\}
has the same distribution as
\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},E_{i},i\in[N]\right\}
and

\xi_{KM,i}=\mathbbm{E}\!\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{(2)}\frac{\delta_{(2)}(t)\,\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\leq t\}}{H^{2}(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)})}v\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{(2)}\right)\,\big|\,\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i}\right).

Overall, by Slutsky’s lemma, (
80
), (
81
), and (
83
), we conclude that

\sqrt{N}\,\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\mathcal{J}}\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{V}_{\mathcal{J}}).

Furthermore, we give a plug-in variance estimator here

\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\mathcal{J}}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}^{\top}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\top},

where

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}=\left(-\frac{\delta_{i}(t)\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq t\}}{\widehat{H}(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i})}+\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})}{1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{i}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{X}_{k}\frac{\delta_{k}(t)\mathbbm{1}{\{\widetilde{T}_{k}\leq t\}}}{\widehat{H}^{2}\left(t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{k}\right)}\hat{v}\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},t\wedge\widetilde{T}_{k}\right),

and
\hat{v}(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},\cdot)
is the estimator of
v(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},\cdot)
, which can be obtained by plugging in the data. Specifically, we have for any positive value
z

\widehat{v}\left(\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{i},z\right)=\widehat{H}(z)\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\widetilde{T}_{i}\leq z,\delta_{i}=0\right\}}{\bar{P}_{N}\left(\widetilde{T}_{i}\right)}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{j}\leq z\wedge\widetilde{T}_{i},\delta_{j}=0\}}{\bar{P}_{N}^{2}(\widetilde{T}_{j})}\right],

(84)

where
\bar{P}_{N}(u)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{T}_{l}>u\}
.
∎

C.3
Technical lemmas

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\mathcal{J}\subseteq[p]
be a fixed group. Under the conditions of Theorem
3.1
and Assumptions
3.4
,
3.5
, and
3.6
, for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
, we have

\left|\boldsymbol{X}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}\right|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(N^{1.5/q}\right).

Proof.

We start by writing

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(N^{1.5/q}\right),

where we use the Markov inequality to bound
\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|

\displaystyle P\left(\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbbm{E}\left(\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|^{q}\right)}{t^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{NK_{\Theta}}{t^{q}},

where
K_{\Theta}
is constant from Assumption
3.5
(iv).
Let
t=N^{1.5/q}
, therefore, we have
\max_{i\in[N]}\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|=O_{P}(N^{1.5/q})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{X}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\boldsymbol{X}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{\infty}+\left|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\boldsymbol{X}\right|_{\infty}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{1}+\left|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(N^{1.5/q}\right),

where by Lemma
3.1
and (
43
), one can see the order of
\left|\boldsymbol{X}\right|_{\infty}\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\right|_{1}
is
o_{P}\left(1\right)
.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\mathcal{J}\subseteq[p]
be a fixed group. Suppose Assumptions
3.4
,
3.5
,
3.6
, and all conditions of Theorem
3.1
are satisfied. If

p^{\frac{1}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}/N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}}=O(\lambda)
and
(p-1)^{\frac{2}{q}}\sqrt{\log p}/N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{q}}=O(\lambda_{j}),j\in\mathcal{J}
, we have for every
j\in\mathcal{J}

\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-e_{j}\right|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}}=O_{P}\left(\frac{p^{2/q}(\log p)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2-2/q}}\right),

where
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
has
1
in the
j
-th element and
0
for others.

Proof.

By (
89
), we have

\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-e_{j}\right|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{j}/\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}.

Since
1/\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}=O_{P}(1)
from (
94
) and
\lambda_{j}=O\left(\frac{p^{2/q}(\log p)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2-2/q}}\right)
, the proof is finished.
∎

Appendix D
Nodewise regression

Notation.

For sets
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, define
\mathcal{S}_{1}-\mathcal{S}_{2}=\{s:s\in\mathcal{S}_{1},s\not\in\mathcal{S}_{2}\}
.

We start by introducing definitions and some inequalities that are useful for the proof.
Define the empirical version of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}
from (
12
) as

\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j},

(85)

and the empirical version of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
from (
6
) as

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})}{\big(1+\exp(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\big)^{2}}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\top}.

(86)

By the definition of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}_{j}
in (
9
), we have
\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}_{j}
. Hence,

\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}\right)}{N}=\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}_{j}}{N},

which shows

\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}}{N}=1,

(87)

since
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}_{j}/\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}
.

By the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions of (
8
), we have

\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}\right)\right|_{\infty}}{N}\leq\lambda_{j},

and dividing each side by
\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}
, using
\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}_{j}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{C}}_{j}/\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}
, we have

\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}\right|_{\infty}}{N}\leq\lambda_{j}/\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}.

(88)

Combining (
87
) and (
88
), one can see that

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}/\hat{\tau}_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},-j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}\right|_{\infty}}{N}

(89)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-e_{j}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}\right|_{\infty}}{N}

\displaystyle=\left|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{j}-e_{j}\right|_{\infty},

where
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
has
1
in the
j
-th element and
0
for others and by definition
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}/N
.

D.1
Proof of Lemma
3.1

Proof.

We first introduce some specific notations. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j,-j}
denote be the
j
-th row of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
after removing its
j
-th column. For the positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
, let
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}
be the
(j,j)
element of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}
be the
j
-th row of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
with
j
-th column removed,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}
be the
j
-th column of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
with
j
-th row removed, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}
be the submatrix of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
with
j
-th row and
j
-th column removed.

By the partitioned inverse formula of the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}
, we know that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j,j}=\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j,-j}=-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j,j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}^{-1}.

It is obvious that

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}^{-1},

where it comes from the solution of the problem (
11
). Hence, we have

\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j,-j}=-\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j,j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta_{0}},j}^{\top}.

(90)

Define

\tau_{j}^{2}:=\frac{1}{N}\mathbbm{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}\right)\right].

(91)

By definition of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\tau_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}\right)/N\right)

(92)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}\right)^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}\right)/N\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}/N\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{\eta},

where the last inequality comes from Assumption
3.5
(iii).
By the partitioned inverse formula, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},-j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-j,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0},j}

(93)
